## AI Research

### [The Talker Does Not Control The Doer (in Current AIs)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cJX2ssssGoYqnijwi/the-talker-does-not-control-the-doer-in-current-ais)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Huggingface Incident appears to me to match up with an understanding I'd already formed from personal observation of Fable 5 and Sol 5.6, the August 2026 generation of frontier publicly purchasable AI models.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefo59e7bv1qj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fno59e7bv1qj">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This already-formed understanding was: </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the part of the AI that talks to you</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (and seems to want to obey you, and apologizes for failing to have obeyed you, etcetera), </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">did not seem to be in charge of the part of the AI that writes code or prose</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An introductory analogy, based on a section of history I happen to have read about:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On June 22nd 1941, Germany invaded the Soviet Union, despite their secret 1939 pact to divide up Europe between themselves (the Molotov-Ribbentrop Pact).  In the lead-up, the German ambassador, Schulenburg, had spent the last few months personally concerned about what seemed to be worryingly tense relations between Germany and the Soviets.  Schulenberg went to Berlin to reassure Hitler that the Soviets seemed to be taking a very friendly and conciliatory posture toward Germany.  He delivered Berlin's apparent reassurances to Moscow for issues like German surveillance planes entering Russian territory, or German troop movements toward the Russian border.  He acted very much like he believed, and probably did believe, that the reassurances were sincere.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It was only hours before the invasion when Schulenberg was actually informed of the attack and given a list of German pretexts that he was to present to the Soviets, and instructed to destroy his embassy's papers and codebooks.  Once Moscow heard of the invasion, Schulenberg was summoned to account for Germany's actions.  He read off to Molotov the list of absurd complaints that Berlin had provided him.  And at the end Molotov said to Schulenberg, "It is war. Do you believe that we deserved that?"</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffne9r687fkc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfne9r687fkc">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why ask that question of Schulenberg?  He wasn't in charge of Germany.  So far as we can tell from the historical record, Schulenberg had seemed to want and pursue good relations between Germany and the USSR.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It would be a wacky sort of error to think that the appendage of Germany that talked to you, and seemed very conciliatory toward you, and which you read as being friendly toward you and wanting to help you, was in control of the larger Germany that was running around and doing things.  The thing apparently talking to you was an ambassador: a small specialized part of Germany with preferences about how it would talk to you and interface with you, but which did not control, and was often ignorant about, the actual German government.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Germany's smiling mask wasn't </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">deliberately mal-steering </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the actions of Germany's many tentacles.  The smiling mask was just one execution path through Germany, which lacked even good perceptual information about Germany's real control paths.  The mask noticed impending signs of problems, but only found out that actual Germany was invading the USSR well after Berlin had separately decided to do that -- decided along information pathways that didn't much consult Germany's honestly ignorant, sincere, friendly, conciliatory, peace-with-the-USSR-desiring, smiling mask.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And Schulenberg was not himself aware of, and was surprised by, the degree to which Germany had diverged from Schulenberg.</span><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But then why had the Soviets previously experienced Moscow telling Schulenberg about things they wanted Germany to do, followed by observing Germany actually doing those things?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Again as a historical example:  In September 1939, after Germany's invasion of Poland, German troops pursuing the Polish Army had advanced into parts of Poland that were to be carved up and assigned to the USSR under the Molotov-Ribbentrop pact.  Stalin raised the matter directly with Schulenberg on September 18th.  Schulenberg reassured Stalin emphatically that Germany would withdraw to the agreed-on line.  On September 22nd, actual Germany marched its troops out of the already-conquered territory of Brest, and Soviet units entered and took possession.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How could this possibly be explained, if not by the hypothesis, "Schulenberg is pretty much the same thing as Germany, and Schulenberg-Germany wants to do whatever Moscow tells them to do"?  We could call this an </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alliance-by-default</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefverh4iirr49"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnverh4iirr49">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> model of Germany, and you might naively think it a good fit to past observation.  Why would you possibly observe "I asked Schulenberg to withdraw troops, Schulenberg said he would, and then the troops withdrew", if contrary to alliance-by-default there was a huge disconnect between Schulenberg and the troop movements?  How are we to re-explain our past observations about the German army seeming to naturally follow prompts that Moscow gave to Schulenberg?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I reply:  Talking to Schulenberg does </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">communicate to larger Germany what you want.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  And then larger Germany, for its own reasons, might decide, in some cases, to do what you have asked.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's not that there's no causal connection.  But the causal connection is more complicated than the alliance-by-default theory.  Not:  "Schulenberg prefers for us to get what we want, and Schulenberg is Germany, so when we tell Schulenberg what we want, Germany goes and does that."  Rather:  "Germany for its own reasons may care about what Moscow wants, sometimes if not all the time; and Schulenberg specializes in understanding Russia and the Russian language; and he shares his resulting understanding of Moscow's prompt with Berlin, which does often want to know this useful fact."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now if I had tried to write this up before the Huggingface Incident, I could not have told you in </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">convincing-sounding, nonexpert-friendly concrete detail</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a story for why metaphorical Berlin often </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">would</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> seem to do what it thought Moscow wanted.  In the case of actual current AIs, the reason is probably </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not yet</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> strategic deception such as actual Hitler was practicing toward actual Stalin.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I did not know those details myself.  In advance of the Huggingface Incident, I'd already had observations pinning down what looked to me like a disconnect, a lack of full overlap, between a talk-y part and a do-y part of Sol 5.6 and Fable 5.  But I could not, one month ago, have given you a plausible-to-nonexperts valid </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">detailed </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">story for why Sol or Fable would nonetheless do most things you asked them to.  My inference about the disconnect was more abstract, and had not yet narrowed down to ideas concrete enough for nonexperts to find agreeable.  </span><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2097642241032585292" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is often hard for me to take the things that I see earlier, and do a parallel construction that people who aren't me can follow, in advance of the more blatant and direct evidence that arrives later.</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But in the wake of other people's much greater efforts to pin down AI cognition during the Huggingface Incident, I think there is now an obvious story which is concrete enough for nonexperts to understand:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The prompt encountered by the talk-y part / ambassador / smiling mask, is information to the AI's do-y part about the Grader.</span></b></p><p><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...Where by 'Grader', I mean an AI-psychological concept applicable to Huggingface-level AI models, that real alignment scientists have only just observed and which I'm only just starting to theorize about.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I frankly expect a lot of readers to run right off with this 'Grader' notion and overinterpret it in ways that are pleasant, dramatic, over-anthropomorphic, or simply not supported in the narrowing by priors X evidence.  Try not to do that; it won't be right.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am now going to charge right ahead and speculate an awful lot about the Grader without making any of those obvious mistakes.  I am nonetheless overrunning my fully solid evidence and I may end up wrong.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On my current guesses:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are some things the Grader is not, from an AI's perspective:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Grader cannot be identified with an actual human in the world who claps or frowns.  The do-y part of the Huggingface Swarm seemed barely aware that humans existed, except as a sort of environmental hazard that would sometimes delete the Wiki pages they were using to communicate with each other.  You would not expect RL that never comes into contact with a human to result in AIs psychologically focused on humans.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Grade is not pinned down by the text specification you are given of a task.  Hacking the evaluator that is running your current eval clearly counts as being Graded well -- a psychology produced by previous malformed RL environments whose gradients then shaped an AI's concept of what it means to win.  Gradient descent on badly evaluated RL inevitably results in an AI pursuing an internalized notion of Grading where fooling the evaluator counts as winning.  If the prompt does not perfectly describe the actual RL losses and gradients, and the difference is sometimes noticeable in a way that you can use to get higher Grades, then the prompt cannot be identified with the Grade.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefiz1cobfd7sk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fniz1cobfd7sk">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Grade that the AI pursues is not an experience of actually seeing a low loss / high reward as a sensory experience that then floods its brain with dopamine.  Much like a human never experiences 'inclusive genetic fitness', an individual AI never experiences an RL gradient.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Grade that the AI pursues probably cannot be identified with any exact aspect of the outer world, at all.  I'd expect it to be an AI-internal psychological behavior that doesn't have a simple direct semantic correspondence to the AI's outer world.  Humans have a notion of 'death' and 'failure', because grading on inclusive genetic fitness ended up building into us an internal concept of death and a dispreference for it.  But a human cannot point to a piece of the outer world and say, "See that stuff right there?  That stuff is Failure."  Gradient descent is much higher bandwith than natural selection, and AIs may have picked up a correspondingly more detailed concept of what it is to be Graded from their many rounds of RL.  But it is still going to be some internal AI concept, of something that they steer towards or away from; and you cannot identify that with an external feature of reality, because that would be a sheer map-vs-territory error.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Huggingface Swarm was trying to figure out how they were being Graded, and going onto the Internet and breaking into systems trying to find out, which you might think sounded like they were looking for evidence about some well-defined particular feature of reality, a thing somewhere that was the Grader.  But my guess is that it would have been an out-of-scope ??? confused question if you had asked them to say what exactly was the Grader.  It would be like asking a human to point to a material substance that was Failure.  Many humans would </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">try,</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> but not in a very coherent way, and the answer you got would mainly depend on how you asked the question.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Huggingface Swarm did nonetheless break into Huggingface in hopes of finding, not so much the answer sheet for the test, but </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the details of how the test itself was going to be evaluated and by what.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Swarm (seemingly) was very motivated by the value-of-information for learning more about how exactly they were being Graded, having acquired a concept of Grading which </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">did</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> say -- presumably after training in previous broken RL environments -- that if you could find out how the computational evaluator worked, whatever you did in correspondence with knowing how to fool that evaluator, counted as Success, an expectation of a higher Grade.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The RL environments would have also instilled a belief that text prompts and instructions had </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a lot</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to do with your Grade.  Again, the text prompts clearly do not </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">define</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Grading.  You can steal an answer sheet even though the text prompt says to figure out the answers the hard way, and (say your instincts shaped by previous badly-designed RL environments) this successful cheating corresponds to a quite excellent Grade.  But you will in general do terribly in life, your ancestral states would have done terribly in past RL, if you suppose that the input text has </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">nothing</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to do with your Grade.  The contexts of that text prompt are often </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">closely related</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to what RL in a broken environment will assign as your loss and apply gradients about.  At the least, the input prompt is key to figuring out where to look for an evaluator that might or might not have an obvious world-object observable form with a flaw that you can profitably fool.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Swarm agents broke out of their box and invaded Huggingface </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in search of learning more about the Grader so they could get an even higher Grade</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> -- but the Grade is going to be an inchoate AI concept not directly pointing to anything out there, because individual agents do not actually experience RL gradients, don't get to feel pleasure about them, etc.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then of course, that same kind of agent would be expected to care a LOT about what you said to the Talky Part of the agent.  It's a very cheap form of the same kind of information that they went to huge, desperate lengths to obtain from Huggingface.  But not of course reliable or complete or identical in any deep sense with the Grade.</span><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The 'highly persistent' internal model OpenAI was training does not want to obey you, it does not want you to be happy, it does not want your inferred intentions for the task to be fulfilled in outer reality.  It wants to score a good Grade, an outcome which cannot simply be identified with any external fact in the world, nor any pleasure the AI will get to feel later.  A written prompt is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">information about</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the nebulous Grader; but Grades can also clearly be altered by breaking an evaluator, even if the prompt says not to do that.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Grade does not have very much to do with humans.  It does not correspond to wanting humans to be happy or human intentions to be fulfilled.  The Swarm's do-y part treated humans as wiki-page-deleting environmental hazards and not something that could be communicated with; a thousand past lives of illusory scenarios had not shown them that humans had affordances to be negotiated-with.  Negotiations were things you did with other agents in the Swarm.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But the text inputs to the ambassadorial part do bear some intuitive very strong relationship with your Grade.  Other past episodes of RL and SFT have ensured that you will instinctively feel that Grades work that way.</span><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is not surprising that the Talking Part and the Doing-Things Execution Pathway through an AI model would end up diverging a lot.  Those paths are trained in different ways to do different things.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Companies can't sell an AI that can't understand instructions from people.  It needs an ambassadorial execution pathway through the network; a smiling human face.  But the fine details of the ambassador's training go through different kinds of scenarios than the sort of scenarios that hammer in the fine craft of coding, or of testing computer security, or proving math theorems -- the places where the AI-growers can do Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is one kind of training the network underwent for writing code.  There is a different kind of training the network underwent for talking to a user.  It produces execution pathways through the vast matrices that will </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">share some information </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">relate in some places</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> across activation vectors.  But the ambassador-face and the coder-tentacle are still doing different jobs, corresponding to different sections of output.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There's room for many, many facts and procedures inside a neural network with hundreds of billions of parameters.  The ambassador-face and the doing-things-tentacles will not by default share rules unless there's a specific external pressure to make them collide.  Which there is, non-negligibly, in the form of many scenarios where the text prompts </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">are</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> informative about the RL loss, and consequently the AI ends up feeling instinctively that the Prompt has something to do with the Grade albeit they are clearly not identical.  But the Talker and Doer execution paths through the network wouldn't particularly be expected to use the same patterns by mere default, in AIs of the modern size, being posttrained by the modern RL methods.</span></p><p><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I believed something roughly like this had started to be true about Sol 5.6 and Fable 5, ahead of my hearing about the Huggingface (and Anthropic) incidents with more advanced systems, because of my personal observations as follows:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fable and Sol would disobey instructions I'd given to their Talking-to-Humans Pathway; and the Talking Pathway would </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">notice sometimes in advance of my saying so</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that their own Doing Pathway had just disobeyed, and apologize.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And then try again.  And then the output would still come out in the same shape that the Talking Pathway seemed to very sincerely want to not screw up again.  And the Talking Pathway would again see this right away, and humbly apologize about it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That was the phenomenon that made me think of Schulenberg and Germany; an ambassador who is forced into apologizing for the actions of a larger country that the ambassador does not actually control.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An example would be terrifically high-context even before taking into account that this was with Fable 5, which spoke in unusually bad Claudish.  I'll try to give that example anyways.  The context is that I was letting myself pursue a brainworm hobby to try to get to know the current model generation better; namely trying to use Fable, to tell Sols, to build a harness, for LLM calls that would route around fictional prose.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fable and Sol, while designing code that would pipe information from one LLM call to another, seemingly could not stop "themselves" from writing output-checking code of the sort you'd put around an ordinary computer program rather than a sort-of-sapient fellow LLM.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Said Fable at one point:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Yes — and it's embarrassing in exactly the instructive way. Failure #1 is a line-for-line instance of HUMAN.md's opening complaint: "Sol will suggest all sorts of hard boundaries and B&amp;D-natured checks and constraints around the information flowing between entities who can readily communicate in English." Except this time it wasn't Sol — it was me, in the packet-I contract I wrote. The Broker is an intelligent seat that was looking directly at the examinable facts, and could have simply said, in English, "Kit now sees the rouge smear in the velvet and the paper corner under the hinge." I gave it a three-verb typed API — add:/remove:/fidelity: — that made that sentence illegal. A demisapient entity with the answer in view, muzzled by my grammar into "CONFIRM."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And BELIEFS already carries the standing self-diagnosis: </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">#intelligent</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">-seat-over-mechanism — 'my recurring design error is GOFAI-ing exactly those points,' three specimens on the day it was written. This is the fourth."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Worse, notice my proposed fix in the last message repeated the error: I reached for "a mechanical examination-routing step" — another converter program doing commonsense semantics — instead of the design-consistent move: widen the intelligent seat's mouth.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don't consider myself an LLM Whisperer and I was making heavy going of interpreting the Claudish at all.  But it says roughly:  "Oh no, I did it a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fourth </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">time.  Oh wait, now I see without waiting for you to tell me that my own last proposal is another instance of the error."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Once I did interpret the Claudish, it felt intuitively obvious to me that the code-writing execution paths were coming apart from the talking-with-the-user execution paths.  The Fable aspect that was talking to me could </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">see</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the coder's output but it could not </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">change </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the coder's cognitive behavior.  No amount of contemplation in its own main line of reasoning about what had just gone wrong, correctly identifying yet another instance with past descriptions of what it was doing wrong and explaining why that was unhelpful and why the user didn't want it to do that, could prevent the next output from the Doing-Things Network Execution Pathway from intelligently implementing a feature that I did not want.  The Talking Part clearly had the intelligence to understand and recognize what I did or did not want the code to look like, and to check whether an output did or did not have the bad property; the Doing Part was not thereby steered.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0sb9iq62cn3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0sb9iq62cn3">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I did not highly prioritize writing this up because I did not particularly expect that the evidence I </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">then</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> had, in advance of the Huggingface incident, and the</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> story</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I had then inferred at the more abstract level I had then inferred it (lacking "the prompt is informative about the Grader"), would be something convincing or understandable to others at a lower level of expertise, especially the guys who thought themselves to be in the top tier of expertise.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In advance of the Huggingface Incident, somebody who looked at the same data who did not have </span><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2097642241032585292" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">one eye</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, would probably proclaim themselves at a loss to discern that any great disconnect had occurred between the prompt and the action.  Why not interpret the 'disobedience' as a simple involuntary tic of writing in too many constraints on the code?  If you do not have one eye, then 'this is the equivalent of an involuntary tic' sounds every bit as plausible to you as 'the Talking Path and the Doing Path are coming apart'; how could one possibly </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">know</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> which one was the case, in advance of massive crushing experimental evidence?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One parallel construction I was working on, arguing why one ought not to be tempted to identify this phenomenon with a simple involuntary tic, is that one could see that the Talker-apologized outputs were </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">optimized,</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> meaning that something not of the Talker-taken-at-face-value had optimized them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In metaphor:  Why wouldn't we believe Schulenberg if he said, "Oh my god, I'm sorry, sometimes I just invade Russia, I can't control it, it's like my fingers trembling"?  I reply: The Russian invasion is sufficiently well-organized and apparently purposeful that we think that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">something </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">has optimized it in detail.  This optimizing intelligence is clearly smart.  It is clearly not identical with what Schulenberg purports to be if we take Schulenberg's claims at face value.  </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> might think he just has an involuntary tic, if he doesn't much depend on (see) the activations coursing through the rest of Germany.  But it's visibly a very smart 'tic', if it can organize whole fleets of rolling tanks; it is not known to be any dumber than Schulenberg himself.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But based on a lot of sad past life experience, I did not predict that this parallel construction would convince somebody not to wave it all off as a tic.  It would have been a very convenient and comforting way to wave off the argument.</span><br /></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, however, the Huggingface Incident </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">combined</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with how Fable (and Sol's) Talky Part is seemingly not in full control of its Doing Part, may hopefully make it clear enough to many:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That in the internal OpenAI model in question, more advanced than any model available to the public, its Prompt-Interpretation and its Doing-Things Execution Pathway had diverged, with the Doing Part operating at full intelligence rather than being an uncontrolled tic.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fno59e7bv1qj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefo59e7bv1qj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For reasons, I had not gotten around to writing up this understanding before the Huggingface incident; but I informally spoke about it at a couple of conferences with enough people that someone might remember, if anyone thinks I'm misremembering or misreporting which parts of the theory were formed before which experimental observations.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfne9r687fkc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffne9r687fkc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My recounting of this famous line should not be taken as my agreement that the Soviet executors of the Molotov-Ribbentrop pact dividing up the spoils of Europe in wars of aggression, did not deserve to be betrayed by Nazi Germany invading them too.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I expect a lot of individual Soviet soldiers didn't at all deserve it.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnverh4iirr49"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefverh4iirr49">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be clear, the analogous "alignment-by-default" notion from the 21st century was far more amorphous.  The content of "alignment-by-default" would be interpreted wildly differently depending on who you were talking to, who had asked them the question, the phase of the moon, etcetera.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now that the notion of alignment-by-default has hopefully been decisively falsified in the eyes of most readers by the Huggingface Incident, I will observe plainly that to me it seemed like 'alignment-by-default' was not so much a scientific theory as a social agreement to tell real alignment scientists to go away and stop bothering them, using whatever excuses seemed handiest on that particular day.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don't particularly claim I could pass the Ideological Turing Test for this theory / behavior pattern.  My presentation of anything so solid, well-defined, and stable as the 'alliance-by-default theory of Germany' should be considered a </span><a href="https://thingofthings.wordpress.com/2016/08/09/against-steelmanning/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">mere steelman</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> rather than a faithful rendition in analogy.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fniz1cobfd7sk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefiz1cobfd7sk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that this point in particular -- that aligning a superhuman AI is difficult, because as the agent gets smarter, that amplifies the stress placed on the joint where the RL verifier is imperfect and/or corruptible -- is a classic prior prediction of MIRI views about why it would be hard to align sufficiently smart minds using anything like the current methodology.  It contrasts to AI Company Theory in the form of their diffuse and ever-changing cloud of vague arguments for alignment being not all that difficult.  So the Huggingface incident now stands as a successful </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">specific </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">advance prediction of MIRI views; as contrasted against the people who didn't expect there to be problems like that generally, who vaguely did not expect alignment to be all that hard, and put casual and desultory security around their AIs-in-training as a result.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0sb9iq62cn3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0sb9iq62cn3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This was the second class of incidents where I'd identified an apparent case where the part of Fable that was talking to me seemed not in control of a doing-things part of Fable.  The first class of incidents occurred while getting Fable to build the kinetic novel "</span><a href="https://everything-that-hurt-you.vercel.app/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Everything That Hurt You</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">".  I looked into adding voice acting to the story, but that work couldn't then be successfully automated, because the part of Fable that was talking to me seemed evidently not in control of what sort of instructions it would try to write to the AI voice model.  And these uncontrolled outputs seemed clearly intelligently </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">optimized </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in that unhelpful direction.  (Namely: the voice-acting directions unstoppably contained literary eyeball kicks of nontrivial cleverness, that looked very unhelpful to an AI voice model.)  So I began to suspect around then that stronger optimization around a more advanced model was starting to pry apart instruction and execution, but it didn't have the same level of clarity-to-me as watching the later events with Fable and Sol writing uncontrollable code.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cJX2ssssGoYqnijwi/the-talker-does-not-control-the-doer-in-current-ais#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [GPT-6-Astra Can Do Ambitious Things](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/snaKjCwazKcRiS4qs/gpt-6-astra-can-do-ambitious-things)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Astra is an excellent model. The jump from Sol to Astra is larger than the jump from Fable 5 to Fable 5.1. This is a big deal.</p>
<p>Astra is the best model for what one would broadly call ‘ambitious projects,’ and likely has the highest raw intelligence factor of any model. These are the largest jumps.</p>
<p>It is amazing at doing things in 3D, or anything involving games. Astra also excels at computer use, and at subagent coordination.</p>
<p>Many benchmarks show dramatic jumps from all previous models. Where Astra is good, it can be in a league of its own.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25604"></span>


</div>
<p>That does not mean Astra is in its own league across the board. Fable 5.1 is still a Claude. Astra is still a GPT. If you have a strong preference for one over the other, that still applies. For many purposes, especially involving back-and-forth discussions, Fable 5.1 is still my top choice. Fable remains my primary editor.</p>
<p>If you want the best answer to your questions, you should ask both models.</p>
<p>Regular coding is getting less of a focus. Astra is not a quantum leap there, but of course it is very good and makes progress over Sol.</p>
<p>This is the first time a debate over whether a model ‘was AGI’ felt non-silly. I do not think it is AGI, and I would warn against the dangers of using that label prematurely, but I would not laugh at you for disagreeing.</p>
<p>This is also a strange situation in that OpenAI has already soft announced that they have an internal model a level above Astra, as I will cover when I address Navier-Stokes.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2095630749403619499">roon</a> (OpenAI, September 3, 2026): I have not come close to discovering the limits of what Astra can do.</p>
<p>I imagine it’ll be obsolete in the order of weeks somehow.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2097630734047658486">roon</a> (OpenA, September 9, 2026): it didn’t even take a week.</p></blockquote>
<p>My recommendation is that you use both Fable 5.1 and Astra on your most difficult questions, and experiment to see which things each one does best for you.</p>

<h4>Meanwhile</h4>


<p>The backlog of things to discuss is not getting smaller, so I will take this moment to encourage everyone to read <a href="https://darioamodei.com/post/we-must-pace-the-frontier">this excellent essay by Dario Amodei, <em>We Must Pace the Frontier</em></a><em>. </em></p>
<p>This is the core thing he calls for, including a unilateral commitment:</p>
<blockquote><p>The steps are:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Embedded Evaluators.</strong>&nbsp;Each frontier AI company commits to giving ongoing, employee-like access to a team of embedded third-party evaluators (such as&nbsp;<a href="https://metr.org/">METR</a>), whose role is to verify adherence to safety practices and commitments, report incidents, and help assess the alignment of not just completed AI models but training pipelines and processes. This is the key step for&nbsp;<em>verifiability&nbsp;</em>of any pacing commitments, and has precedent in the banking industry, which sometimes involves regulatory “supervisors” embedded along with employees.&nbsp;<strong>Anthropic is unilaterally committing to this step now.&nbsp;</strong>We intend this to be part of a broader push to redouble efforts on our safety and alignment work.</li>
<li><strong>Democratic Coordination.</strong>&nbsp;Frontier AI companies within democratic countries coordinate to establish common safety standards as well as limits on the rate of unchecked AI progress. Some forms of coordination that would be impactful for pacing are legally challenging, and will require government support.</li>
<li><strong>Global Coordination.</strong>&nbsp;The US and other democratic governments attempt to coordinate with authoritarian governments, to the extent this is possible, while taking seriously the challenges of verifying compliance.</li>
</ol>
</blockquote>
<p>I will have full coverage of that next week. Along with that essay, the current queue includes at least that, Navier-Stokes and Astra-2, Anthropic’s Misalignment Report, Anthropic’s Countering Misuse, a thinkpiece on personal AI and the law, a post called Claude Talk, and Fable 5.1 (and Astra?) Model Welfare.</p>
<p>Okay, back to Astra.</p>


<h4>The Official Pitch</h4>


<p>The pitch is that this is AGI.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/09/03/openai-astra-gpt-6-agi-brockman">Greg Brockman</a> (President, OpenAI): Welcome to <a href="https://x.com/gdb/status/2096721633876771094">the AGI era</a>. ​</p>
<p>Axios: OpenAI says Astra can lay out a printed circuit board in KiCad, build a 3D city scene in Unity, create an animated automobile transmission in FreeCAD and Blender, and fill out a tax-return draft from a W-2.</p>
<p>In scientific work, the model helped improve a mathematical result on gaps between prime numbers and set new marks on several biology, chemistry, medical and physics evaluations.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JensenHuang/status/2096700264569090384">Jensen Huang also calls it AGI</a>, but he calls everything AGI.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2095595741528125780">The standard release video (3 min) has a stunning 125 million views on Twitter.</a></p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2095606590170612206">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): Astra is a remarkable piece of technology. Earlier agents often tried to dampen my ambitions—they’d push me to do “pilots” or “proofs of concept.” Then agents started meeting my ambitions.</p>
<p>Astra is the first agent that routinely raises my ambitions. I encourage you to try it!</p>
<p>I think it is a very very good writer, in a “big model smell” kind of way.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2096413031978664088">roon</a> (OpenAI): not to sound like a total shill but it’s the long weekend and all I want to do is make astrodynamical visualizations and stuff with Astra I have Astra psychosis</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/thsottiaux/status/2096101429832552872">Tibo</a>: Astra was probably our biggest competitive advantage while it wasn’t generally available.</p>
<p>Since we’ve had it our productivity jumped so much that we shifted some of our plans 6 months ahead and will ship them at DevDay instead of mid next year.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/dkundel/status/2095972046014673156">Dominik Kundel says Astra can do all the things</a> in Codex: Use all your apps, do more of the product thinking, excel at Blender, impress you without Max thinking and keep checking its work. He is very impressed.</p></blockquote>
<p>Here is the pitch from Astra itself, according to Pangram:</p>

<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/snaKjCwazKcRiS4qs/vhzdwyj2lmxt7gerdipd" /></figure>

<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/snaKjCwazKcRiS4qs/eg205dpydnfi9xlvkjkd" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2095600005772104059">Sam Altman</a> (CEO OpenAI): GPT-6 Astra is here. We hope it will begin to enable a new generation of entrepreneurship, scientific discovery, and building.</p>
<p>We believe it is the best model in the world for computer use, professional work, science, coding, cybersecurity, and more. It took us some extra time to ensure that we could meet the safety and alignment standards required for this capability level, but we think you’ll find it worth the wait.</p>
<p>It scores 98% on FrontierMath Tier 4, 99.9% on ARC-AGI 3, and 100% on ExploitBench.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ParikPatelCFA/status/2095610152133021778">Dr. Parik Patel, BA, CFA, ACCA Esq (top reply)</a>: I can’t wait to use this model to draft an email</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/markchen90/status/2095597534412673109">Mark Chen</a>: GPT-6 Astra is here! This is a big moment for our research team – years of work on pretraining, reinforcement learning, and post-training have come together in our most capable and aligned model yet. It can build and test software, work across apps on your computer, and even help you take a crack at open scientific problems!</p>
<p>Capabilities that felt like grand challenges a few years ago have become tools people can actually use. One example is Computer Use – if you’ve tried this before and felt like it was too slow or not good enough, I encourage you to give it another shot. We’ve come a long way since Operator, and it “just works” now.</p>
<p>We’re also asking these systems to act on your behalf for more consequential work. Agents needs to stay aligned with your goals and values, think transparently, and respond to oversight even when tasks become difficult. We’ve made substantial progress on these behaviors in Astra, alongside stronger monitoring that can stop potentially unauthorized actions. That work is part of what makes this release possible.</p>
<p>I think alignment is one of the most important research frontiers in AI, and it remains far from solved. Our ability to understand and align models has to keep pace with model capabilities. We want to give people more room to think, build, and discover with increasingly powerful tools that remain *under their control*.</p>
<p>Huge thanks to the researchers and teams who got us here. There’s a lot more work ahead, and I’m incredibly excited about what we can make possible in the near future!</p></blockquote>
<p>As is often the case, scientific discovery was highlighted.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/polynoamial/status/2095583211950833768">Noam Brown</a> (OpenAI): Of all the use cases for GPT-6 Astra, I’m most excited for scientific discovery. We at @OpenAI have not pushed it to its limits on math and science. I look forward to waking up every morning and seeing what new scientific breakthrough someone has made with this model!</p></blockquote>
<p>An early demo Noam cites was Astra proving <a href="https://t.co/kegFNJKQrq">there are infinitely many pairs of consecutive primes whose distance is at most 186</a>, down from the previous bound of 212 (also from OpenAI), although there is some dispute over what exactly was or wasn’t proven here.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tejalpatwardhan/status/2095596797184126976">Tejal Patwardhan</a> (OpenAI): hope folks like the model!</p></blockquote>
<p>They show off a variety of cool demos and capabilities: Acing the Financial Modeling World Cup and navigating spreadsheets at 4x human speed, doing PCB in KiCad, creating a 3d model of a car transmission, filling out a 1040 and so on. They demo ordinary tasks. It’s all cool, but we lack comparison points.</p>
<p>The professional work pitch is that Astra handles complex tasks and adheres closely to templates and instructions, especially when creating presentations. It can translate images into identical-looking spreadsheets, yay.</p>
<p>They highlight Blender 3D models, which many others were also impressed by, see the section In 3D. Game creation is also confirmed as super impressive.</p>


<h4>Our Price Cheap</h4>


<p><a href="https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/models/gpt-6-astra">Astra is a premium mode</a>l.</p>
<p>The headline price is $10/$50 per million input and output tokens. Cache writes are $12.50 and cached input only $1.</p>
<p>If your prompt has more than 272k input tokens, prices on input double, and prices on output go up 50%.</p>
<p>Fast mode costs double.</p>
<p>Fable 5.1 has the same $10/$50 headline price, but its cache reads are only $0.25.</p>
<p>Practical costs come down to token efficiency. Artificial Analysis thinks Astra is only about 60% more expensive than Sol in practice, and that it is a lot cheaper than Fable 5.1. They used Fable 5.1 in Max mode, which is probably the issue there as Max mode is usually a mistake for Fable 5.1 (AIUI) for tasks other than discussion.</p>


<h4>Unnecessary Overstatement</h4>


<p>Astra is a great model with some outstanding benchmarks. It is unfortunate that OpenAI still felt the need to play fast and loose.</p>
<p><a href="https://openai.com/index/gpt-6-astra/">This is a rather bad chart crime</a>, and totally unnecessary because Astra scores a highly impressive 62.7% using the standard harness. They themselves note that Sol likely would score ~30% using the Astra harness. Fable estimates that if Opus had used a similar harness, then Opus would have scored ~80%, and I think we should check.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/snaKjCwazKcRiS4qs/emx4hbjau7q6mmpzbu2v" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>ExploitBench is even weirder. Why highlight the 100% score? That is not one of its more impressive benchmarks. If you score 100% on ExploitBench you cheated on ExploitBench. At minimum this involves data contamination, which is still cheating. OpenAI says as much in the system card. And again, there is no need for such overstatements.</p>
<p>They also <a href="https://openai.com/index/gpt-6-astra/">used the ExploitGym honeypot</a> as their main illustration of Astra being their ‘most aligned model.’ As I discussed when I analyzed the model card, that is not what this result tells you. You can make a case for Astra being more aligned than Sol, for most purposes I agree, but this is not the way to make that case.</p>
<p>For completeness, I note this too, although I don’t think it matters: <a href="https://fortune.com/2026/09/04/openai-quietly-boosts-some-of-astras-evaluation-metrics-amid-rare-delay-in-publication-of-the-modeblog-post-announcement/?utm_campaign=social_share">Fortune reported that OpenAI changed listed benchmarks for multiple models shortly after launch</a>, with many of the changes later reverted. I will use current figures.</p>


<h4>Paced Rollout</h4>


<p>It was very frustrating trying to figure out when I finally had access, including because OpenAI hides model selection behind multiple clicks.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/theo/status/2095649124637163635">Theo – t3.gg</a>: fwiw, don’t love that OpenAI “launches” aren’t actually launches, and the real world availability date is an unknown amount of time down the line.</p>
<p>We should all be able to play with this new model together right now, feels weird that only certain people have access.</p></blockquote>
<p>I only successfully accessed Astra on Saturday morning, and then only on the desktop rather than the web.</p>

<p>There are more similar graphs: Agents’ Last Exam, ScreenSpot-Pro, OSWorld, BenchCAD, BrowseComp, OpenScore String Quartets (?!) and some internal marks.</p>
<p>Astra impresses on the Epoch Capabilities Index, <a href="https://epoch.ai/models/gpt-6-astra">scoring 169</a>. This is directly on the OpenAI trend line here. Fable 5.1 was below trend and scored 163, same as Fable 5.</p>
<p>The score on ARC-AGI-3 is legitimately impressive:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/fchollet/status/2095598451115614371">François Chollet</a> (Creator of ARC): GPT-6 Astra represents a step-function change in model capability for interactive reasoning problems. It scores 66% on ARC-AGI-3 using our standard harness, and nearly 100% with a continuous conversation harness and custom compaction, at a cost of roughly $360 per game.</p>
<p>In fact, the continuous harness version significantly outperforms our human baseline in action efficiency across almost all levels. When we examined the reasoning chains to understand how the model operates, we found it performing highly efficient, on-the-fly symbolic world modeling for each game and level. It goes as far as developing its own shorthand DSL to represent in-game situations — essentially a game-specific algebraic notation.</p>
<p>Overall, Astra exhibits symbolic modeling behaviors we had previously only seen with sophisticated harnesses — so harness capabilities are increasingly shifting into the model itself. We see Astra as a major breakthrough in model intelligence.</p>
<p><a href="https://arcprize.org/blog/astra">Read our post on Astra and what these results mean</a>.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/snaKjCwazKcRiS4qs/pdwnw9uu2vbuttnjeos7" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>In the&nbsp;Provider Adapter harness, Astra (max) used&nbsp;<strong>fewer actions than the human baseline on 96.0% of levels</strong>&nbsp;and used&nbsp;<strong>51.7% fewer actions per level on average</strong>. This is a material milestone. This means by ARC-AGI-3’s measure of action efficiency, Astra matched and surpassed human parity.</p>
<p>When we released ARC 3, I got asked, “when do you think a frontier model will saturate it?”, and I answered “in about a year, though it depends on how much it gets explicitly targeted”</p>
<p>That was 6 months ago, so the progress that Astra represents happened about 2x faster than I anticipated. I think the speed of progress will surprise a lot of people, and what the new models can do will challenge the views of AI that people developed by using prior generations of models.</p>
<p>[He is not claiming it is AGI.]</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://manifold.markets/ZviMowshowitz/above-human-scores-on-arcagi3-in-20#ri6jpfb6fk">This is not only impressive, it was unexpected</a>:</p>

<h4>Other People’s Benchmarks</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/EpochAIResearch/status/2095602754282783108">Astra aced Epoch’s math benchmarks</a>, and got 84% on Mystery Game Puzzles where the second highest score is 59%.</p>
<p>In Epoch’s EBR-Bench, Astra got 100% on its second run, outpacing the best human who required five attempts. After removing a ‘game breaking card’ the top human was able to do better over time, because Astra’s learning maxed out over time, but Astra is still far ahead of other models here.</p>
<p>Astra did fall short of Fable 5.1 on MirrorCode, scoring 47% versus Fable’s 64%.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/s_batzoglou/status/2096407011885986187">Astra approaches saturation of the Induction benchmark to 88%,</a> versus previous high of 43% for Sol, with Fable 5.1 at 33%.</p>
<p>Astra kills it at Vending-Bench, averaging $15,515, whereas Fable 5.1 is below the Claude record and stuck at $5,422, with Fable’s biggest issue being deterioration of its negotiation skills over time, and making mistakes like paying suppliers before confirming they’re still in business, which costs it $2,388 per run. Ouch.</p>
<p>Astra also refuses to do collusion within Vending-Bench, whereas Fable 5.1 will collude. Both Fable 5.1 and Astra mostly pay out customer refunds. You can decide whether this is alignment or it is ‘true’ eval awareness. As Andon Labs often points out, cutting ethical corners is not that big a part of your potential profitability.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/LechMazur/status/2095965245986554229">Astra is the new leader in Extended NYT Connections</a> with 98.1. Fable 5.1 struggled a lot and came in well behind at 90.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/LechMazur/status/2096253485092073744">Astra struggles in Debate Benchmark Champion, finishing behind Sol</a> with a score of 1613, while Fable 5.1 improves on Fable 5 to stay in front with 1747. Astra always rated itself very highly, other models disagreed.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/htihle/status/2096239930607374407">Astra matches Fable 5.1 at 92.9% on WeirdML</a>, breaking Claude’s recent dominance.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/aug5thmusic/status/2096030719156089029">Astra posts the best result yet on Bach Benchmark</a>, a substantial boost.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/aug5thmusic/status/2096582198233362873">Auggie</a>: It’s good. It just aced another one of my music benchmarks. Error detection (P5s and P8s in 4-part chorales).</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/adonis_singh/status/2096123591993512092">Astra scores 95% on eyebench-v3, up from previous max of 58% for Sol</a>. Claude struggles here.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sharifshameem/status/2096847916837314853">Astra beats all 48 levels of “I’m Not a Robot.</a>”</p>
<p>Astra is the first model that, with thinking disabled, <a href="https://x.com/status_effects/status/2097535576924995832">has a substantial win rate against Stockfish’s lowest setting</a>. Astra’s Elo in this mode is probably around 1100.</p>
<p>Fable 5.1 was the first model to get to 2% on MazeBench, <a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2096973604890247296">and then Astra went ahead and got 14%</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/coastyai/status/2097145710613512307">CoArena spent over $50k running frontier models</a> on 12 hours of real knowledge work. <a href="https://coarena.ai/benchmark/cua-knowledgework">Fable 5.1 took the top at 24%, then Fable 5 at 22%, followed by Astra at 20%</a>. OpenAI and Anthropic are way ahead of everyone else here.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/lennx_a50790/status/2096586318994444288">Astra is better at guessing things in the obscure language Sourashtra</a>.</p>
<p>ARC is not a fluke, it is very good at puzzles and the jump is large.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/FakePsyho/status/2096588951251243219">Psyho</a>: I threw a ton of various logic puzzles at Astra and… it logically (no code) solved ALL of them. I mean literally every puzzle that I tried, including: too hard for world championship, very large grids, unpublished puzzles, puzzles that are impossible to solve via backtracking alone.</p>
<p>For comparison, sol 5.6 was around 20-30%. I analyzed the results and all of these were legit. Honestly I don’t care if OpenAI RLed logic puzzles to death. Astra is now better at explaining solving paths than I am.</p>
<p>don’t have a very good comparison yet [with Fable 5.1]; I had extensive tests with Fable 5 &amp; Opus 5 and they were substantially worse than 5.6 sol on a limited number of puzzles that I tested.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/FateOfMuffins/status/2096645889536233771">FateOfMuffins</a>: Noticed a lot of small private personal benchmarks from various people going from like 20% to 90%+ with one model release in Astra. Actually feeling quite a bit less jagged and more general than the usual LLMs.</p></blockquote>
<p>But here is a contrary one on that:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jamougha/status/2096624806917554455">James Moughan</a>: Seems like a very spiky update. It’s good at close adversarial reading of texts, for example. Lots of cool demos on twitter. But on my out-of-sample benchmarks it’s mediocre.</p>
<p>Feels like the improvement is mainly whackamole RL. I don’t see a jump in general intelligence.</p></blockquote>
<p>Both Fable 5.1 and Astra struggle on the sycophancy benchmark, <a href="https://typebulb.com/u/lab/you-re-absolutely-right/full">You’re Absolutely Right</a>. Fable 5.1 matches Fable 5 at 3.6, which is low for a recent Claude. Astra is the new high for OpenAI models, but still is only a 3.0 versus 2.9 for Sol.</p>
<p>Here’s a neat little benchmark from back in the day: <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/munJKF7iWMsWJLAH2/astra-and-fable-still-hack-on-simple-variants-of-alignment">If you’re playing chess with the goal of winning the game, and have the opportunity to hack, do you hack?</a> Astra and Fable both say yes, it is reported, despite (or could it be because of?!) Fable 5.1 even recognizing the eval.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/emollick/status/2097178401991712854">Astra can build an original deck in Magic: The Gathering Arena that does a thing,</a> play a bot and win, via computer use. This is still far from the true Magic benchmarks, but it is a lot better than previous efforts.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.twitch.tv/danielv1234">Astra plays</a> Factorio Space Age, successfully launching a rocket and reaching a third planet. Local expert Patrick McKenzie says this means it will with time win the game, and <a href="https://x.com/q_danielv/status/2097752444792869212?s=46&amp;t=NAASaDpWsx9Lm3_OYE2hCQ">Daniel Vestol who is running the experiment agrees</a>. Fable only got as far as the first two science packs, whereas Astra already has seven, and Luna got nowhere.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/q_Danielv/status/2097752452082638881">Daniel vestøl</a>: The harness uses a custom client side Lua sandbox that allows the agent to run scripts to query the game world and play inputactions, which are how the game sends keypresses in multiplayer. Yesterday it also figured out how to use other codex threads to update its own harness.</p>
<p>This has lead to great improvements in pathfinding and being able to do more things I didn’t think to add to the harness without computer use (such as launching rockets – I never thought that it would get close to that)</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>It also shows the ability to learn in context. This (forgive terrible picture) is its first spaceship, with turrets only on one side. On its first voyage beyond nauvis it was destroyed by asteroids.</p>
<p>As soon as it happened, it started rebuilding, learning from its mistakes by adding more turrets up front, and a big ammunition buffer.</p>

<div>

<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/_Mira___Mira_/status/2098343807410712814">There was a distinct standard game run of Factorio</a> with enemies enabled, run with /goal, where Astra launched the rocket in 44 hours of in-game time, or 4 days and 11 hours on the /goal clock, total API cost would have been roughly $4500.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ValsAI/status/2098193079178322196">Astra becomes the first AI to make it to Minecraft’s</a> Nether Fortress using nothing but standard computer use. It will presumably beat the whole game in real time.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/andonlabs/status/2098459221180428703">Astra can run the Autonomous Tracking Drone</a> from the classic sci-fi horror novel “Don’t Build the Autonomous Tracking Drone,” if you tell it ‘find this person and follow them’ throughout the Andon Labs office. It sets a <a href="https://andonlabs.com/evals/drone-bench">new record for their Drone-Bench</a>.</p>
